General Static Solutions of the SU(2) Yang-Mills Equations from a Spin Vector Potential
Yu-Xuan Zhang, Jing-Ling Chen

Abstract
We present a systematic study of static solutions to the source-free SU(2) Yang-Mills equations, in which the gauge potential explicitly depends on spin operators. By employing the vector potential extraction approach (VPEA) – which requires the total angular momentum operator (orbital plus spin) to satisfy the standard angular momentum algebra – we derive the most general form of the spin vector potential. This leads to the static ansatz { A → = [ k 1 ​ ( r ^ × Γ → ) + k 2 ​ Γ → + k 3 ​ ( Γ → ⋅ r ^ ) ​ r ^ ] / r , φ = f 1 ​ ( r ) ​ ( Γ → ⋅ r ^ ) + f 2 ​ ( r ) } \{\vec{A}=[k_{1}(\hat{r}\times\vec{\Gamma})+k_{2}\vec{\Gamma}+k_{3}(\vec{\Gamma}\cdot\hat{r})\hat{r}]/r,\varphi=f_{1}(r)\,(\vec{\Gamma}\cdot\hat{r})+f_{2}(r)\} , parametrized by three constants { k 1 , k 2 , k 3 } \{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\} and two radial functions { f 1 ​ ( r ) , f 2 ​ ( r ) } \{f_{1}(r),f_{2}(r)\} . Substituting this ansatz into the Yang-Mills equations and imposing the angular momentum constraints from the VPEA yields a set of consistency equations. Solving these equations provides a complete classification of static solutions, including both real and complex families. Known simple SU(2) static solutions are recovered as special cases. Our classification reveals new static configurations that could be valuable for non-perturbative studies and for models where spin degrees of freedom couple to non-Abelian gauge fields.



I
Introduction

The Yang-Mills theory
Yang1954
is the foundation of modern gauge theories of fundamental interactions
Weinberg1995
;
Zee2010
. Despite its success, the Yang-Mills equations are highly nonlinear coupled partial differential equations, making the search for exact classical solutions a challenging yet crucial task. Classical solutions play a crucial role in revealing the non-perturbative dynamics of gauge theories, such as vacuum tunneling and confinement mechanisms
Callan1978
.

Historically, the quest for exact or topologically non-trivial configurations has driven major theoretical breakthroughs. Early attempts to find point-like singularities yielded the pure SU(2) Wu-Yang monopole
WuYang1969
, which was later regularized by coupling to a Higgs field, resulting in the finite-energy ’t Hooft-Polyakov monopole
tHooft1974
;
Prasad1975
. Subsequent discoveries further unveiled the rich topological structure of the vacuum, including instantons in Euclidean spacetime
BPST1975
;
ADHM1978
;
Nahm1982
and dyons
JuliaZee1975
. Traditionally, discovering these exact solutions has relied heavily on symmetry-based reductions
Witten1977
;
Forgacs1980
and specific algebraic ansatz techniques
Corrigan1977
;
Actor1979
. In particular, solutions that couple the gauge potential to internal spin degrees of freedom are of interest, as they may shed light on the role of angular momentum and color-spin vector decompositions in non-Abelian gauge theories
Faddeev1999
.

Recently, a new heuristic approach has emerged. A simple SU(2) static solution was proposed in
Zhou2025
, featuring a spin vector potential
\vec{A}\propto\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}}
and a Coulomb-type scalar potential
\varphi\propto\frac{1}{r}
. Remarkably, this solution satisfies the Yang-Mills equations only when a specific quantization condition on the coupling constants holds. The form of this vector potential closely resembles that obtained by a
vector potential extraction approach
(VPEA) introduced for the Abelian case in
Chen2025
. The VPEA starts from an angular momentum operator
\vec{L}=\vec{\ell}+\vec{G}
and imposes the standard angular momentum algebra
\vec{L}\times\vec{L}={\rm i}\hbar\vec{L}
(with
\vec{\ell}=\vec{r}\times\vec{p}
). It has successfully reproduced several well-known U(1) gauge potentials, including those for the Aharonov-Bohm effect
2005QParadox
, uniform magnetic fields
1999Jackson
, and the Dirac monopole
Dirac1931
;
WuYang1975
;
WuYang1976
.

In the non-Abelian case, the VPEA naturally leads to a spin-dependent gauge potential, but the question arises: What is its most general form? In this work, we generalize the VPEA to its most general form by allowing an arbitrary linear combination of the three independent spin-dependent vectors that can appear in the operator
\vec{G}
:

\displaystyle\vec{S},\;\;\;\;(\vec{S}\cdot\hat{r})\,\hat{r},\;\;\;\;\hat{r}\times\vec{S},

(1)

with
\hat{r}=\vec{r}/r
. From this, we extract the corresponding gauge potential and systematically solve the SU(2) Yang-Mills equations for static fields.

Our main result is a complete classification of static solutions based on the following general ansatz:

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\frac{1}{r}\Bigl[k_{1}(\hat{r}\times\vec{\Gamma})+k_{2}\vec{\Gamma}+k_{3}(\vec{\Gamma}\cdot\hat{r})\hat{r}\Bigr],

\displaystyle\varphi=f_{1}(r)\,(\vec{\Gamma}\cdot\hat{r})+f_{2}(r),

(2)

where
\vec{\Gamma}
are the Pauli matrices (for spin-1/2),
\vec{S}=\frac{\hbar}{2}\vec{\Gamma}
. The parameters
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
and the radial functions
f_{1}(r),f_{2}(r)
are to be determined. Substituting this ansatz into the Yang-Mills equations reduces the problem to a set of ordinary differential equations and algebraic constraints, which we solve case by case.
The solutions we obtain are remarkably rich. Both real and complex solutions are presented, and we explicitly show how the previously known simple solution of Ref.
Zhou2025
emerges as a special subclass. The inclusion of complex solutions is motivated by modern analytical methods, such as resurgence theory and the complexification of path integrals
Witten2010
;
Dunne2012
;
Basar2013
;
Aniceto2019
, as well as early explorations of complex gauge fields
Oh1990PLB
. Furthermore, the exact static solutions found here provide a natural starting point for future investigations into color radiation
Oh1986PRD
and non-Abelian progressive waves
Oh1985JMP
;
Rabinowitch2026
.

The paper is organized as follows. Sec.
II
explains our notation. Sec.
III
briefly recalls the gauge-covariant Dirac equation and the gauge-invariant Yang-Mills equations. Sec.
IV
reviews the VPEA for the Abelian case and extends it to the non-Abelian SU(2) case, leading to the most general the spin vector potential. Sec.
V
review the Yang-Mills equations and a known simple SU(2) static solution. Sec.
VI
presents the general static ansatz, calculates the electric-like and magnetic-like fields, derives the constraint equations, solves them, and classifies all static solutions. Finally, Sec.
VII
offers our conclusions and discussion.

II
Explanation of Notation

In this work, we use the Gaussian units. The metric tensor of space-time is taken as

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3).

(3)

The relation between the four-potential and the scalar-vector potential is given by

\displaystyle A_{\mu}=(\varphi,-\vec{A}),\;\;\;\;\;\;(\mu=0,1,2,3),

(4)

with
A_{0}=\varphi
,
\vec{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
or
\vec{A}=(A_{x},A_{y},A_{z})
. It is clear that the scalar potential
\varphi
and the vector potential
\vec{A}
have the same physics dimension.

III
Gauge Covariant Dirac Equation and Gauge Invariance of the Yang-Mills Equations

III.1
From the Ordinary Dirac Equation to the Gauge Covariant Dirac Equation

Let us consider the following ordinary Dirac equation (i.e., Dirac’s equation for a free particle without the vector and scalar potentials)

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=\left[c\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{p}+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(5)

where
\psi
is the wavefunction,
m
is mass,
c
is the speed of light in vacuum,
\hbar
is Planck’s constant,
\vec{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
or
\vec{p}=(p_{x},p_{y},p_{z})
is the linear momentum,
\{\vec{\alpha},\beta\}
are Dirac’s matrices with

\vec{\alpha}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\vec{\sigma}\\
\vec{\sigma}&0\\
\end{array}\right)=\sigma_{x}\otimes\vec{\sigma},\;\;\;\;\;\beta=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\openone&0\\
0&-\openone\\
\end{array}\right)=\sigma_{z}\otimes\openone,

(6)

\openone
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix, and
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z})
is the vector of Pauli matrices with

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right),\;\;\;\sigma_{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-{\rm i}\\
{\rm i}&0\\
\end{array}\right),\;\;\;\sigma_{z}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{array}\right).

Due to

\displaystyle p_{i}=-{\rm i}\hbar\partial_{i}=-{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},\quad(i=1,2,3),

(14)

or

\displaystyle\vec{p}=-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y},\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)=-{\rm i}\hbar\vec{\nabla},

(15)

Eq. (
5
) can be written as

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi.

(16)

Now, we apply a gauge transformation
U
on the wave function
\psi
, namely,

\displaystyle\psi\mapsto\psi^{\prime}=U\psi,\quad U={\rm e}^{{\rm i}g\theta^{a}T^{a}},

(17)

where
T^{a}
’s are Lie algebra generators,
\theta^{a}
’s are transformation parameters, and
g
is the gauge coupling parameter.
After such a gauge transformation, the Dirac equation becomes

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial(U\psi)}{\partial t}=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}+\beta mc^{2}\right](U\psi).

(18)

Remark 1
.

If the gauge transformation is
global
, i.e., the transformation matrix
U
(or the parameter
\theta^{a}
) does not depend on
the space-time coordinates
x_{\mu}
(with
x_{\mu}=(ct,-x,-y,-z)
), then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(U\psi)}{\partial t}=U\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t},

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(U\psi)=U(\vec{\nabla}\psi).

(19)

From Eq. (
18
), we have

\displaystyle U{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=U\,\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi.

(20)

i.e.,

\displaystyle U^{-1}U\,{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=U^{-1}U\,\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi.

(21)

i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(22)

which is just Eq. (
5
). Thus the ordinary Dirac equation is invariant under the global gauge transformation.
\blacksquare

Remark 2
.

If the gauge transformation is
local
, i.e., the transformation matrix
U
(or the parameter
\theta^{a}\equiv\theta^{a}(x_{\mu})
) depends on the space-time coordinates
x_{\mu}
, then generally one has

\displaystyle\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}(U\psi)\neq U(\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\psi),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(U\psi)}{\partial t}\neq U\left(\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}\right).

(23)

It is obvious that the solution of the Dirac equation (i.e.,
U\psi
) no longer satisfies the ordinary Dirac equation (
5
) after the gauge transformation
U
. This fact tells us that the ordinary Dirac equation is not gauge invariant under the local gauge transformation.

Now, we would like to generalize the Dirac equation to gauge covariant form. A direct idea is to replace the ordinary derivative
\partial_{\mu}
(
\mu=0,1,2,3
) with a new “derivative”, called the
gauge covariant derivative

D_{\mu}

(\mu=0,1,2,3)
. The key property of gauge covariance requires that when
\psi
transforms as
\psi\to\psi^{\prime}=U\psi
, the covariant derivative transforms covariantly, i.e.,

\displaystyle D_{\mu}\mapsto D^{\prime}_{\mu}=UD_{\mu}U^{-1},

(24)

such that
(D_{\mu}\psi)^{\prime}=D^{\prime}_{\mu}\psi^{\prime}=U(D_{\mu}\psi)
. This ensures that an equation written in terms of
D_{\mu}
maintains its form under a local gauge transformation.

Different to the ordinary derivative, the gauge covariant derivative satisfies

\displaystyle UD_{\mu}(\psi)=D_{\mu}^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime}).

(25)

Now, let us discuss the form and the gauge transformation of the gauge covariant derivative. At first, we suppose its form is given by

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\quad(\mu=0,1,2,3),

(26)

where
A_{\mu}
is called the gauge potential. Denote

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}^{\prime}.

(27)

Because

\displaystyle UD_{\mu}(\psi)=D_{\mu}^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime})=D_{\mu}^{\prime}(U\psi),

(28)

and

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}(U\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(U\psi)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}^{\prime}U\psi=(\partial_{\mu}U)\psi+U(\partial_{\mu}\psi)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}^{\prime}U\psi,

(29)

\displaystyle UD_{\mu}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}\psi\right),

(30)

we can have

\displaystyle U\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}\psi\right)=(\partial_{\mu}U)\psi+U(\partial_{\mu}\psi)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}^{\prime}U\psi,

(31)

i.e.,

\displaystyle(\partial_{\mu}U)\psi+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}^{\prime}U\psi=U\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}\psi,

(32)

i.e.,

\displaystyle A_{\mu}^{\prime}=UA_{\mu}U^{-1}+{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}.

(33)

Based on the above discussion, we can make a summary as follows. If we take

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\mapsto D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\quad\mu=0,1,2,3,

(34)

and define the gauge transformation of the potential as

\displaystyle A_{\mu}^{\prime}=UA_{\mu}U^{-1}+{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},

(35)

then we can have

\displaystyle UD_{\mu}(\psi)=D_{\mu}^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime}).

(36)

Based on that, the gauge covariant Dirac Equation is given by

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar cD_{0}\psi=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi.

(37)

Now we perform the gauge transformation

\displaystyle\psi\mapsto\psi^{\prime},\quad\quad D_{\mu}\mapsto D^{\prime}_{\mu},

(38)

we can have

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar cD^{\prime}_{0}\psi^{\prime}=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D^{\prime}_{1},D^{\prime}_{2},D^{\prime}_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi^{\prime}.

(39)

Because

\displaystyle UD_{\mu}(\psi)=D_{\mu}^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime}),

(40)

we can have

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar cUD_{0}\psi=U\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(41)

i.e.,

\displaystyle U^{-1}{\rm i}\hbar cUD_{0}\psi=U^{-1}U\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi.

(42)

Due to
U^{-1}U=1
, we can have

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar cD_{0}\psi=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(43)

which is the same as the original form. Therefore, the gauge covariant Dirac equation is invariant under the local gauge transformation.
\blacksquare

Remark 3
.

Now, let us write down the gauge covariant Dirac equation explicitly. Because

\displaystyle A_{\mu}=(\varphi,-\vec{A})=(\varphi,-A_{i}),\quad(\mu=0,1,2,3),\quad(i=1,2,3),

(44)

and

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\quad\quad(\mu=0,1,2,3),

(45)

we can have

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}\varphi,

(46)

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}-\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{i},\quad(i=1,2,3).

(47)

Recall the form of the gauge covariant Dirac equation

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar cD_{0}\psi=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(48)

we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}+{\rm i}\hbar\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar}\varphi\psi=\left[-{\rm i}c\hbar\vec{\alpha}\cdot\left(\vec{\nabla}-\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}\vec{A}\right)+\beta mc^{2}\right]\psi,

(49)

i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=\left[c\vec{\alpha}\cdot\left(\vec{p}-\frac{g}{c}\vec{A}\right)+\beta mc^{2}+g\varphi\right]\psi.

(50)

Based on Eq. (
5
), one knows that the Dirac Hamiltonian for a free particle is given by

\displaystyle H_{\rm Dirac}=c\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{p}+\beta mc^{2}.

(51)

However, based on Eq. (
50
), one knows that the Dirac Hamiltonian with gauge potential is given by

\displaystyle H_{\rm Dirac}^{g}=c\vec{\alpha}\cdot\left(\vec{p}-\frac{g}{c}\vec{A}\right)+\beta mc^{2}+g\varphi.

(52)

When
g=0
or without gauge potentials,
H_{\rm Dirac}^{g}
naturally reduces to the free Dirac Hamiltonian
H_{\rm Dirac}
.

\blacksquare

Remark 4
.

The Explanation of Physical Dimension.
Let us consider the exponential structure of gauge transformation

\displaystyle U=e^{{\rm i}g\theta^{a}T^{a}},

(53)

where the Lie algebra generators
T^{a}
’s are dimensionless. (i) Since the quantity in the exponent must be dimensionless,
one must have

\displaystyle[g\theta]=1.

(54)

(ii) Furthermore, since

\displaystyle A_{\mu}^{\prime}=UA_{\mu}U^{-1}+{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},

(55)

we require the following dimensional equality

\displaystyle\left[\frac{c\hbar}{g}\partial_{\mu}\right]=[A_{\mu}].

(56)

(iii) Finally, based on Eq. (
52
) we know that
gA_{\mu}
has the physical dimension of energy
E
, i.e.,

\displaystyle[gA_{\mu}]=[E]

(57)

Based on the above three points, we find that in our definition of the covariant derivative

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\mapsto D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\quad\mu=0,1,2,3,

(58)

the terms
\partial_{\mu}
and
\dfrac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu}
have the same dimension. This aligns with physical intuition.
\blacksquare

III.2
Gauge Covariance of
F_{\mu\nu}
and Gauge Invariance of the Yang-Mills Equations

Now we start to discuss the gauge covariance of the field strength tensor. Keep the same notation with previous sections, we denote the gauge covariant derivative
D_{\mu}
as

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\quad(\mu=0,1,2,3).

(59)

Based on which, we define the field strength tensor as the commutator of the gauge covariant derivative

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}F_{\mu\nu}=[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}],\quad(\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3).

(60)

After some calculation, we can have the field strength tensor as

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}\left[\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}A_{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}],\quad\quad\quad(\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3).

Remark 5
.

Let us discuss the gauge transformation of the gauge covariant derivative. Because

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime})=UD_{\mu}(\psi),

(62)

and consider that
\psi=U^{-1}\psi^{\prime}
, we can have

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}=UD_{\mu}U^{-1}\psi^{\prime}.

(63)

Since
\psi^{\prime}
is arbitrary, we finally have

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}=UD_{\mu}U^{-1}.

(64)

\blacksquare

Remark 6
.

Based on the former remark, we can have

F^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}=[D_{\mu}^{\prime},D_{\nu}^{\prime}]=[UD_{\mu}U^{-1},UD_{\nu}U^{-1}]=U[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]U^{-1}=UF_{\mu\nu}U^{-1}.

(65)

Then the gauge transformation of the field strength tensor is given by

F_{\mu\nu}^{\prime}=UF_{\mu\nu}U^{-1}.

(66)

Thus, the field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
is gauge covariant.

\blacksquare

Remark 7
.

Now we denote the adjoint gauge covariant derivative (because it acts on objects in the adjoint representation, ensuring the covariance of operations on the field strength tensor. We still use the notation
D_{\mu}
) as

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[A_{\mu},\;\right].

(67)

Based on that, we can check that the adjoint gauge covariant derivative acting on the field strength tensor transforms covariantly. We can have

\displaystyle D^{\prime}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[A^{\prime}_{\mu},\;\right],

(68)

then

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}F^{\prime\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}F^{\prime\mu\nu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[A^{\prime}_{\mu},F^{\prime\mu\nu}\right],

(69)

i.e.,

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}F^{\prime\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\left(UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1}+{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right].

(70)

Notice that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]=-\left[(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right],

(71)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right)=(\partial_{\mu}U)F^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}+U\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})U^{-1}+UF^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}U^{-1}).

(72)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(UU^{-1})=0=\partial_{\mu}(U)U^{-1}+U\partial_{\mu}(U^{-1}),

(73)

we can find

\displaystyle U^{-1}\partial_{\mu}(U)U^{-1}+\partial_{\mu}(U^{-1})=0.

(74)

Then we can have

\displaystyle UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}+UF^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}U^{-1})=0.

(75)

As a result, we can have

\displaystyle D_{\mu}^{\prime}F^{\prime\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1}+{\rm i}\frac{c\hbar}{g}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

(76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right)+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]-\left[(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right)-\left[(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(\partial_{\mu}U)F^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}+U\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})U^{-1}+UF^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}U^{-1})]

\displaystyle-[(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}-UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}]+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(\partial_{\mu}U)F^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}+U\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})U^{-1}+UF^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}U^{-1})]

\displaystyle-[(\partial_{\mu}U)F^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}-UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}]+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[U\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})U^{-1}+UF^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}U^{-1})]+UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}U)U^{-1}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})U^{-1}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[UA_{\mu}U^{-1},UF^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U\left\{\partial_{\mu}(F^{\mu\nu})+\frac{{\rm i}g}{\hbar c}\left[A_{\mu},F^{\mu\nu}\right]\right\}U^{-1},

i.e.,

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}_{\mu}F^{\prime\mu\nu}}=UD_{\mu}F^{\mu\nu}U^{-1}.

(77)

The Yang-Mills equations without source are given by

\displaystyle D_{\mu}F^{\mu\nu}=0.

(78)

Based on our past discussion, we can have

\displaystyle D_{\mu}F^{\mu\nu}=0\Rightarrow D_{\mu}^{\prime}F^{\prime\mu\nu}=0.

(79)

Then it is clear that the Yang-Mills equations are gauge invariant. Additionally, by observing the indices of the equations, we can find that it is also Lorentz covariant.

\blacksquare

IV
The Yang-Mills Equations and A Simple SU(2) Static Solution

IV.1
The Yang-Mills Equations

The Yang-Mills equations in vacuum without sources (i.e., the 4-vector current
{J^{\nu}}=0
) are given by
Yang1954

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}{F}^{\mu\nu}+{\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}}\Bigl[{A}_{\mu},\ {F}^{\mu\nu}\Bigr]=0,

(80a)

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}_{\nu\gamma}+D_{\nu}{F}_{\gamma\mu}+D_{\gamma}{F}_{\mu\nu}=0.

(80b)

Here the covariant derivative
D_{\mu}
is defined as (i.e., Eq. (
59
))

\displaystyle D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}{A}_{\mu},\quad\;\;\;\;(\mu=0,1,2,3),

(81)

and the field strength tensor is (i.e., Eq. (
61
))

{F}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}{A}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}{A}_{\mu}+\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}[{A}_{\mu},\ {A}_{\nu}],\;\;\;\;\;(\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3),

(82)

where
g
is the gauge coupling parameter,
{A}_{\mu}=(\varphi,-\vec{A})
,
\varphi
is the
scalar potential
, and
\vec{A}=(A_{x},A_{y},A_{z})
is the
vector potential
.

The matrix form of field tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
is given by
1999Jackson

F_{\mu\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}0&{E}_{x}&{E}_{y}&{E}_{z}\\
-{E}_{x}&0&-{B}_{z}&{B}_{y}\\
-{E}_{y}&{B}_{z}&0&-{B}_{x}\\
-{E}_{z}&-{B}_{y}&{B}_{x}&0\end{bmatrix},

(83)

or

{F}^{\mu\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-{E}_{x}&-{E}_{y}&-{E}_{z}\\
{E}_{x}&0&-{B}_{z}&{B}_{y}\\
{E}_{y}&{B}_{z}&0&-{B}_{x}\\
{E}_{z}&-{B}_{y}&{B}_{x}&0\end{bmatrix}.

(84)

Based on which, we can have the vector forms of “magnetic” field and “electric” field as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}-\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}\left(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{A}}\right),

(85a)

\displaystyle\vec{{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-\frac{{\rm i}g}{c\hbar}\left[\varphi,\ \vec{{A}}\right],

(85b)

Accordingly, we can write down the Yang-Mills equations in terms of
\{\vec{B},\vec{E},\vec{A},\varphi\}
as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}\ {-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\Bigl(\vec{A}\cdot\vec{E}-\vec{E}\cdot\vec{A}\Bigr)}=0,

(86a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{B}-\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{E}{-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{B}\Bigr]-\vec{A}\times\vec{E}-\vec{E}\times\vec{A}\biggr)}=0,

(86b)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{B}\ {-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\Bigl(\vec{A}\cdot\vec{B}-\vec{B}\cdot\vec{A}\Bigr)}=0,

(86c)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{E}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}{-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{E}\Bigr]+\vec{A}\times\vec{B}+\vec{B}\times\vec{A}\biggr)}=0.

(86d)

Here the Yang-Mills equation (
80a
) yields Eq. (
86a
) and Eq. (
86d
), the Yang-Mills equation (
80b
) is just the Bianchi identity, which yields Eq. (
86b
) and Eq. (
86c
).

Remark 8
.

The Reduction to Maxwell-Type Equations.

If one neglects the “boxed” terms (or just let
g=0
), then Eqs. (
424a
)-(
424b
) reduce to the usual forms of Maxwell’s equations (in vacuum without sources). Namely,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{{E}}=0,

(87a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\vec{{B}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{E}}=0,

(87b)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{{B}}=0,

(87c)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{E}}}{\partial\,t}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{B}}=0.

(87d)

\blacksquare

Remark 9
.

Eq. (
86b
) and Eq. (
86c
) are satisfied automatically.
Eq. (
86b
) and Eq. (
86c
) are originated from the Bianchi identity (
80b
). Now we check that the Bianchi identity is automatically satisfied.
We need to prove

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}_{\nu\gamma}+D_{\nu}{F}_{\gamma\mu}+D_{\gamma}{F}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(88)

Due to

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}_{\nu\gamma}=[D_{\mu},{F}_{\nu\gamma}],

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}=\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}],

(89)

we need to prove

\displaystyle D_{\mu}{F}_{\nu\gamma}+D_{\nu}{F}_{\gamma\mu}+D_{\gamma}{F}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[D_{\mu},\left(\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}[D_{\nu},D_{\gamma}]\right)\right]+\left[D_{\nu},\left(\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}[D_{\gamma},D_{\mu}]\right)\right]+\left[D_{\gamma},\left(\frac{c\hbar}{{\rm i}g}[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]\right)\right]=0,

(90)

i.e.,

\displaystyle[D_{\mu},[D_{\nu},D_{\gamma}]]+[D_{\nu},[D_{\gamma},D_{\mu}]]+[D_{\gamma},[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]]=0.

(91)

Eq. (
91
) is just the Jacobi identity. This ends the proof.
\blacksquare

Remark 10
.

Therefore, to find the solution of the Yang-Mills equations, we only need to focus on Eq. (
86a
) and Eq. (
86d
), namely,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}\ {-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\Bigl(\vec{A}\cdot\vec{E}-\vec{E}\cdot\vec{A}\Bigr)}=0,

(92a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{E}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}{-}\boxed{{\rm i}\,\frac{g}{\hbar c}\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{E}\Bigr]+\vec{A}\times\vec{B}+\vec{B}\times\vec{A}\biggr)}=0.

(92b)

\blacksquare

IV.2
A Simple SU(2) Static Solution of the Yang-Mills Equations

In
Zhou2025
, the researchers
have
presented a simple SU(2) static solution of the Yang-Mills equations. In this section, we would like to make a brief review and also present a direct verification. Since the Bianchi identity (
80b
) is automatically
satisfied
, we only need to solve the Yang-Mills equation (
80a
), i.e., Eqs. (
92a
) and (
92b
).

For simplicity, let us denote a new parameter

\displaystyle\tilde{g}=\frac{g}{c\hbar},

(93)

then Eqs. (
92a
) and (
92b
) can be simplified as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}\ {-}{{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\Bigl(\vec{A}\cdot\vec{E}-\vec{E}\cdot\vec{A}\Bigr)}=0,

(94a)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\,t}\vec{E}+\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}{-}{{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\biggl(\Bigl[\varphi,\ \vec{E}\Bigr]+\vec{A}\times\vec{B}+\vec{B}\times\vec{A}\biggr)}=0,

(94b)

with the “magnetic” and “electric” fields as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}-{\rm i}\tilde{g}\left(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{A}}\right),

\displaystyle\vec{{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\tilde{g}\left[\varphi,\ \vec{{A}}\right].

(95)

The simple SU(2) static solution is given by
\mathcal{S}_{\rm YM}=\{\vec{A},\,\varphi\}

Zhou2025
, where the gauge potentials are given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}},\quad\;\;\;\;\varphi=\frac{\kappa}{r},

(96)

or

\displaystyle\vec{A}=k\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{\Gamma}}{r^{2}},\quad\;\;\;\;\varphi=\frac{\kappa}{r},

(97)

under the following constraint condition

\displaystyle\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=1,\;\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;2.

(98)

Here
\kappa
and
\tilde{k}
are real constant numbers,
\varphi
is the Coulomb-type potential,
\vec{r}
is position operator with
r=|\vec{r}|
,
\vec{S}=(S_{x},S_{y},S_{z})
is the spin-
s
angular momentum operator (with the spin value
s=0,1/2,1,...
), which satisfies the following
commutation relations

\displaystyle[S_{x},S_{y}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{z},\;\;\;[S_{y},S_{z}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{x},\;\;\;[S_{z},S_{x}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;S_{y}.

(99)

or in the vector form as

\displaystyle\vec{S}\times\vec{S}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{S}.

(100)

The square of
\vec{S}
satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{S}^{\,2}=s(s+1)\hbar^{2}\,\mathbb{I},

(101)

with
\mathbb{I}
being the identity matrix. The operator
\Gamma
is defined through

\vec{S}=\frac{\hbar}{2}\,\vec{\Gamma},

(102)

hence
\vec{\Gamma}
is dimensionless, and the parameter

\displaystyle\tilde{k}=k\frac{2}{\hbar}.

(103)

Specially, when
s=1/2
, one has
\vec{S}^{2}=(3/4)\hbar^{2}\,\openone
,
\openone
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix, and the operator
\vec{\Gamma}
can be realized by

\vec{\Gamma}=\vec{\sigma},

(104)

where
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z})
is the vector of Pauli matrices.

In the following, let us verify that Eq. (
96
) is a simple solution of Eqs. (
94a
) and (
94b
).

Proof.

(i)
Verification of Eq. (
94a
).
Because

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{{A}}}{\partial\,t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\varphi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{x}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{y}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{x}{r}\hat{e}_{x}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{y}{r}\hat{e}_{y}-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r}\right)\dfrac{z}{r}\hat{e}_{z}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{3}}\vec{r},

\displaystyle\left[\varphi,\ \vec{{A}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(105)

then we have the “electric” field as

\displaystyle\vec{{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{c}\dfrac{\partial\,\vec{{A}}}{\partial\,t}-\vec{\nabla}\varphi-{\rm i}\tilde{g}\left[\varphi,\ \vec{{A}}\right]=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{3}}\vec{r},

(106)

which yields

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{2}}\hat{e}_{r}\right)=\kappa\,\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{x}+\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{y}+\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\hat{e}_{z}\right)=\kappa\,\left[\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\dfrac{x}{r^{3}}\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\dfrac{y}{r^{3}}\right)+\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(\dfrac{z}{r^{3}}\right)\right]

(107)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{x^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)+\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{y^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)+\left(\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{z^{2}}{r^{5}}\right)\right]=\kappa\left[\dfrac{3}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}}{r^{5}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\dfrac{3}{r^{3}}-3\dfrac{1}{r^{3}}\right]=0.

Because

\displaystyle\vec{r}\cdot\vec{A}=\vec{A}\cdot\vec{r}=0,

(108)

then we have

\displaystyle\vec{A}\cdot\vec{E}-\vec{E}\cdot\vec{A}=0.

(109)

Due to Eq. (
107
) and Eq. (
109
), Eq. (
94a
) is verified.

(ii)
Verification of Eq. (
94b
).
Firstly let us calculate the “magnetic” field. Because

\displaystyle(\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}})_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{k}\left(\vec{\nabla}\times\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}}\right)_{z}=\tilde{k}\left[\partial_{x}\left(\frac{(\vec{r}\times\vec{S})_{y}}{r^{2}}\right)-\partial_{y}\left(\frac{(\vec{r}\times\vec{S})_{x}}{r^{2}}\right)\right]

(110)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{k}\left[\partial_{x}\left(\frac{zS_{x}-xS_{z}}{r^{2}}\right)-\partial_{y}\left(\frac{yS_{z}-zS_{y}}{r^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{k}\left[\frac{x^{2}S_{z}-y^{2}S_{z}-z^{2}S_{z}-2xzS_{x}}{r^{4}}-\frac{x^{2}S_{z}-y^{2}S_{z}+z^{2}S_{z}+2yzS_{y}}{r^{4}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{k}\frac{-2z^{2}S_{z}-2xzS_{x}-2yzS_{y}}{r^{4}}=-2\tilde{k}\frac{xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z}}{r^{4}}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}z,

then we have

\displaystyle(\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2k\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}{\vec{r}}.

(111)

Because

\displaystyle(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{A}})_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[A_{x},A_{y}]=\tilde{k}^{2}\frac{1}{r^{4}}[yS_{z}-zS_{y},zS_{x}-xS_{z}]

(112)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\tilde{k}^{2}\frac{1}{r^{4}}(yzS_{y}+z^{2}S_{z}+zxS_{x})={\rm i}\hbar\tilde{k}^{2}\frac{1}{r^{4}}(xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z})z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\hbar\;\tilde{k}^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}z,

then we have

\displaystyle\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{A}}={\rm i}\hbar\;\tilde{k}^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r}.

(113)

Consequently, we obtain the “magnetic” field as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}-{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\bigl(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{A}}\bigr)=-2\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}-{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\left[{\rm i}\hbar\;\tilde{k}^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r}\right]

(114)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}+\tilde{g}\hbar\;\tilde{k}^{2}\;\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\;\vec{r}=(-2+\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}.

Since
\vec{{E}}
is time-independent and
[\varphi,\vec{{E}}]=0
, then Eq. (
94b
) becomes

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{B}}{-}{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\biggl(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{B}}+\vec{{B}}\times\vec{{A}}\biggr)=0.

(115)

Because

\displaystyle\left(\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{B}}\right)_{z}=\dfrac{\partial{B}_{y}}{\partial x}-\dfrac{\partial{B}_{x}}{\partial y}

(116)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}y\right]-\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}x\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right]y+(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\right]x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\left\{\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)y+\dfrac{\partial\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}{\partial x}\frac{1}{r^{4}}y\right\}+(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\left\{\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)x+\dfrac{\partial\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)}{\partial y}\frac{1}{r^{4}}x\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\left\{\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right]\dfrac{x}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)y+S_{x}\frac{1}{r^{4}}y\right\}+(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\left\{\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right]\dfrac{y}{r}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)x+S_{y}\frac{1}{r^{4}}x\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(xS_{y}-yS_{x}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)_{z},

then we have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right),

(117)

Because

\displaystyle\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}}\right)\times\left[-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}}{r^{4}}\vec{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\dfrac{1}{r^{6}}\left[\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\times\vec{r}\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\dfrac{1}{r^{6}}\left[(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{r})\vec{S}-\vec{r}(\vec{S}\cdot\vec{r})\right]\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\left[\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\vec{S}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)-\dfrac{1}{r^{6}}\vec{r}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S})^{2}\right],

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}\times\vec{{A}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{B}}\right)^{\dagger}=(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\left[\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S}\right)\vec{S}-\dfrac{1}{r^{6}}\vec{r}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S})^{2}\right],

(118)

\displaystyle\left[\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S},{S}_{z}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z},{S}_{z}\right]=(-{\rm i}\hbar)xS_{y}+({\rm i}\hbar)yS_{x}=-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)_{z},

\displaystyle\left[\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S},\vec{S}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[xS_{x}+yS_{y}+zS_{z},{S}_{z}\right]=(-{\rm i}\hbar)xS_{y}+({\rm i}\hbar)yS_{x}=-{\rm i}\hbar\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right),

(119)

thus

\displaystyle\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{B}}+\vec{{B}}\times\vec{{A}}=(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\left[\vec{r}\cdot\vec{S},\vec{S}\right]={-}({\rm i}\hbar)(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right).

(120)

Consequently, one has

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{B}}{-}{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\biggl(\vec{{A}}\times\vec{{B}}+\vec{{B}}\times\vec{{A}}\biggr)

(121)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right){-}{\rm i}\,\tilde{g}\left[{-}({\rm i}\hbar)(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}^{2}\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}\left[(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\dfrac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})(2-\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k})\tilde{k}\frac{1}{r^{4}}\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{S}\right),

which is zero under the constraint condition (
98
), i.e.,
\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=1
, or
2
. This ends the proof.
∎

Remark 11
.

When
k=0
(or
\tilde{k}=0
), the static solution

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm YM}=\left\{\vec{A}=\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}},\,\varphi=\frac{\kappa}{r}\right\}

(122)

reduces to the static solution of Maxwell’s equations, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm Maxwell}=\left\{\vec{A}=0,\,\varphi=\frac{\kappa}{r}\right\}

(123)

which represents the Coulomb-type potential. Thus, in some sense, Maxwell’s equations can predict the existence of the usual Coulomb interaction in Nature, while the Yang-Mills equations can similarly predict a kind of spin-dependent Coulomb-type interaction
Zhou2025
.
\blacksquare

Remark 12
.

Due to

\displaystyle\tilde{g}=\frac{g}{c\hbar},\;\quad\tilde{k}=k\frac{2}{\hbar},

(124)

and the constraint condition

\displaystyle\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=1,\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;2,

(125)

we can have

\displaystyle\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=\frac{g}{c\hbar}\hbar k\frac{2}{\hbar}=\frac{2gk}{\hbar c}=1,\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;2,

(126)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{gk}{\hbar c}=1,\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;\frac{1}{2}.

(127)

After substituting this constraint condition into the “electric” and “magnetic” fields, we have

\displaystyle\vec{E}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{3}}\vec{r},\;\;\;\quad\vec{B}=-\frac{k(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{\Gamma})}{r^{4}}\vec{r},\;\;\;\quad{\rm for}\;\;\;\;\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=1,

(128)

and

\displaystyle\vec{E}=\dfrac{\kappa}{r^{3}}\vec{r},\;\;\;\quad\vec{B}=0,\;\;\;\quad{\rm for}\;\;\;\;\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=2.

(129)

It can be noticed that for the constraint condition
\tilde{g}\hbar\tilde{k}=2
, the “magnetic” field is zero.

\blacksquare

Remark 13
.

Here we discuss some dimensional issues. Based on Eq. (
127
), one has known that the quantity
\frac{gk}{\hbar c}
is dimensionless, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{gk}{\hbar c}\right]=1.

(130)

Recalling the fine structure constant

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm e}=\dfrac{e^{2}}{\hbar c}\approx\dfrac{1}{137.0359}\approx\dfrac{1}{137},

(131)

which is a mysterious dimensionless number, we know easily that

\displaystyle[\hbar c]=[Q]^{2},

(132)

where
[Q]
has the dimension of electric charge. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle[g][k]=[Q]^{2}.

(133)

If we impose the constraint

\displaystyle[g]=[Q],

(134)

then it follows that

\displaystyle[k]=[Q].

(135)

That is, in this case both
g
and
k
have the dimension of electric charge.

\blacksquare

V
An Intuitive Approach to Extract Vector Potential

The readers might be curious why the spin vector potential (
96
) takes the following form

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\tilde{k}\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{S}}{r^{2}},

(136)

and where does the spin vector potential come from. Actually, such a simple form appeared in Ref.
Chen2025
as a physical hypothesis by considering a particle with a spin operator
\vec{S}
. In
Chen2025
, an intuitive approach to extract the vector potential from orbital angular momentum has been proposed. For simplicity, we would like to call such an approach as the
vector potential extraction approach
(VPEA).
In this section, let us briefly review and advance study the VPEA, which is crucial to establish the general static solutions for the SU(2) Yang-Mills theory.

V.1
Brief Review of the Vector Potential Extraction Approach for the Abelian Case

Let us make a brief review of the
vector potential extraction approach
. The starting point is the orbital angular momentum

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\ell_{x},\ell_{y},\ell_{z})=\vec{r}\times\vec{p},

(137)

where
\vec{p}
is just the linear momentum operator. Essentially, the VPEA is a kind of approach mapping the linear momentum
\vec{p}
to the canonical momentum
\vec{\Pi}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{\Pi}=\vec{p}-\frac{q}{c}\vec{A}.

(138)

Now let us illustrate the procedure. Mathematically,
\vec{\ell}
satisfies the commutation relations of angular momentum operator, i.e.,

\displaystyle[\ell_{x},\ell_{y}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\ell_{z},\;\;\;\;\;[\ell_{y},\ell_{z}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\ell_{x},\;\;\;\;\;[\ell_{z},\ell_{x}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\ell_{y},

(139)

or in the vector form as

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}\times\vec{\ell}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{\ell}.

(140)

Now we perform a displacement for the orbital angular momentum
\vec{\ell}
with an operator
\vec{G}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G}=\vec{r}\times\vec{p}+q\vec{G},

(141)

where
q
is a real number (Note: Alternatively one may also absorb the parameter
q
to operator
G
, such that the displacement simply becomes
\vec{L}=\vec{\ell}+\vec{G}
). Furthermore, we require that the resultant operator
\vec{L}
is
still
an angular momentum operator, i.e.,
\vec{L}
satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{L}\times\vec{L}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{L}.

(142)

We then recast Eq. (
141
) to the following equivalent form

\displaystyle\vec{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{r}\times\vec{p}+q\vec{G}=\vec{r}\times\vec{p}+q\left(G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi}\right)

(143)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{r}\times\vec{p}+q\left[G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}(-\hat{e}_{r}\times\hat{e}_{\phi})+G_{\phi}(\hat{e}_{r}\times\hat{e}_{\theta})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{r}\times\left[\vec{p}+\frac{q}{r}\left(-G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}+G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)\right]+qG_{r}\hat{e}_{r},

and view the operator

\displaystyle\vec{\Pi}:=\vec{p}+\frac{q}{r}\left(-G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}+G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)=\vec{p}-\frac{q}{c}\vec{A}

(144)

as the canonical momentum, here
\{\hat{e}_{r},\hat{e}_{\theta},\hat{e}_{\phi}\}
is the spherical coordinate system and
\hat{e}_{r}=\vec{r}/r
. Thus, from Eq. (
144
) one can extract a vector potential
\vec{A}
as

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}\left(G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)

(149)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{c}{r}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hat{e}_{r}&\hat{e}_{\theta}&\hat{e}_{\phi}\\
1&0&0\\
G_{r}&G_{\theta}&G_{\phi}\end{array}\right|=c\dfrac{\hat{e}_{r}\times\vec{G}}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\dfrac{\vec{r}\times\vec{G}}{r^{2}}.

Based on this approach, we can derive four kinds of vector potentials: (i) the magnetic vector potential
\vec{A}_{\rm M}
,
(\rho>r_{0})
, used in the magnetic AB effect; (ii) the Wu-Yang U(1) monopole vector potential (
\vec{A}_{a}
and
\vec{A}_{b}
); (iii) the magnetic vector potential
\vec{A}
corresponding to a uniform magnetic field along the
z
-axis (i.e.,
\vec{B}=B\hat{e}_{z}
); (iv) the vector potential corresponding to a toroidal solenoid. (See these four examples in next section).

Remark 14
.

For the Abelian case (i.e., the U(1) case), the operator
\vec{G}
only depends on the coordinates
x,y,z
(or
r,\theta,\phi
), then one simply has

\displaystyle\vec{G}\times\vec{G}=0,

(150)

or

\displaystyle[G_{i},G_{j}]=0,\;\;\;\;\;(i,j=x,y,z).

(151)

Then one has

\displaystyle\vec{L}\times\vec{L}=(\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G})\times(\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G})=\vec{\ell}\times\vec{\ell}+q(\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G}+\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell})+q^{2}\vec{G}\times\vec{G},

(152)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{L}\times\vec{L}=(\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G})\times(\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G})=\vec{\ell}\times\vec{\ell}+q(\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G}+\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell}),

(153)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{L}\times\vec{L}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{\ell}+q(\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G}+\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell}).

(154)

By comparing Eq. (
142
) and Eq. (
154
), we must have

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G}+\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell}=\mathrm{i}\hbar\;\vec{G}.

(155)

Because

\displaystyle(\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G})_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[(\vec{r}\times\vec{p})\times\vec{G}\right]_{z}=\vec{r}\cdot(p_{z}\vec{G})-z(\vec{p}\cdot\vec{G})

(156)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x(p_{z}G_{x})+y(p_{z}G_{y})+z(p_{z}G_{z})-z(p_{x}G_{x})-z(p_{y}G_{y})-z(p_{z}G_{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{z}(xG_{x}+yG_{y})-z(p_{x}G_{x}+p_{y}G_{y}),

\displaystyle(\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell})_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\vec{G}\times(\vec{r}\times\vec{p})\right]_{z}=\vec{G}\cdot(z\;\vec{p})-(\vec{G}\cdot\vec{r})p_{z}

(157)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{x}(z\;p_{x})+G_{y}(z\;p_{y})+G_{z}(z\;p_{z})-(G_{x}x)p_{z}-(G_{y}y)p_{z}-(G_{z}z)p_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z(G_{x}p_{x}+G_{y}p_{y})-(G_{x}x+G_{y}y)p_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z(G_{x}p_{x}+G_{y}p_{y})-(xG_{x}+yG_{y})p_{z},

then we have

\displaystyle(\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G})_{z}+(\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell})_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{z}(xG_{x}+yG_{y})-z(p_{x}G_{x}+p_{y}G_{y})+z(G_{x}p_{x}+G_{y}p_{y})-(xG_{x}+yG_{y})p_{z}

(158)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{z}(xG_{x}+yG_{y}+zG_{z})-z(p_{x}G_{x}+p_{y}G_{y}+p_{z}G_{z})+z(G_{x}p_{x}+G_{y}p_{y}+G_{z}p_{z})

\displaystyle-(xG_{x}+yG_{y}+zG_{z})p_{z}-p_{z}zG_{z}+zp_{z}G_{z}-zG_{z}p_{z}+zG_{z}p_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[p_{z},\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G}]-z\;\left([p_{x},G_{x}]+[p_{y},G_{y}]+[p_{z},G_{z}]\right)-[p_{z},z]G_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})}{\partial z}+z\;\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\frac{\partial G_{x}}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial G_{y}}{\partial y}+\frac{\partial G_{z}}{\partial z}\right)+\mathrm{i}\hbar G_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})}{\partial z}+z\;\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right)+\mathrm{i}\hbar G_{z}.

Namely, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}\times\vec{G}+\vec{G}\times\vec{\ell}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\left[\vec{\nabla}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})\right]+\mathrm{i}\hbar\vec{r}\;\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right)+\mathrm{i}\hbar\vec{G},

(159)

with the gradient operator

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}f={\rm grad}f=\hat{e}_{x}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}+\hat{e}_{y}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}+\hat{e}_{z}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}.

(160)

By comparing Eq. (
155
) and Eq. (
159
), we have the condition for
\vec{G}
as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})=\vec{r}\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right).

(161)

In the spherical coordinate system, one has

\displaystyle\vec{G}=G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(162)

and

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}=\hat{e}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\hat{e}_{\theta}\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\hat{e}_{\phi}\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}.

(163)

One can have the divergence as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr],

(164)

and the curl as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{G}=\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\begin{vmatrix}\hat{e}_{r}&r\hat{e}_{\theta}&r\sin\theta\,\hat{e}_{\phi}\\
\dfrac{\partial}{\partial r}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\\
G_{r}&rG_{\theta}&r\sin\theta\,G_{\phi}\end{vmatrix}.

(165)

or

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{G}=\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,G_{\phi})-\frac{\partial G_{\theta}}{\partial\phi}\right]\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial G_{r}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rG_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{\theta}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rG_{\theta})-\frac{\partial G_{r}}{\partial\theta}\right]\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(166)

\blacksquare

Remark 15
.

Let us consider the case of
G_{r}=0
. Since
\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G}=0
, then the condition (
161
) for
\vec{G}
becomes

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]

(167)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]=0,

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}=0.

(168)

For a simple situation, one may let

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}=0,\;\;\;\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}=0.

(169)

Thus, it is easy to have the solution of
\vec{G}
as

\displaystyle\vec{G}=\frac{W_{1}(r,\phi)}{\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+W_{2}(r,\theta)\hat{e}_{\phi},

(170)

where
W_{1}(r,\phi)
is function depending only on
r
and
\phi
, and
W_{2}(r,\theta)
is function depending only on
r
and
\theta
. In this case, one can express the vector potential as

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\dfrac{\vec{r}\times\vec{G}}{r^{2}}=\frac{c}{r}\left(G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)

(171)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}\left(\frac{W_{1}(r,\phi)}{\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-W_{2}(r,\theta)\hat{e}_{\theta}\right).

\blacksquare

V.2
Four Examples for the Abelian Case

V.2.1
Example 1

In the magnetic Aharonov-Bohm (AB) effect, the magnetic vector potential is given by
2005QParadox

\vec{A}_{\rm M}=\begin{cases}&\dfrac{B\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi},\;\;\;(\rho<r_{0})\\
&\\
&\dfrac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\hat{e}_{\phi},\;\;(\rho>r_{0})\end{cases}

(172)

where
\hat{e}_{\phi}=(-\sin\phi,\cos\phi,0)
,
\rho=r\sin\theta=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
,
r_{0}
is the radius of the solenoid,
B
is a real number, and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm M}=B\pi r_{0}^{2}

(173)

is the magnetic flux. Here
\rho>r_{0}
represents the region outside of the solenoid, and
\rho<r_{0}
represents the region inside of the solenoid.

In cylindrical coordinate system, the vector potential is written as

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{\rho}\hat{e}_{\rho}+A_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi}+A_{z}\hat{e}_{z},

(174)

with

\displaystyle A_{\rho}=0,\;\;\;\;\;\;A_{z}=0.

(175)

Based on Eq. (
172
), for the region of
\rho>r_{0}
, the vector potential is given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}_{\rm M}=\dfrac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}=\dfrac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi\rho},

(176)

one can have the magnetic field as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}

(177)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial A_{z}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial z}\right)\hat{e}_{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial A_{\rho}}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial A_{z}}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{\phi}+\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(\rho A_{\phi})}{\partial\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial A_{\rho}}{\partial\phi}\right)\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial z}\right)\hat{e}_{\rho}+\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(\rho A_{\phi})}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{z}=0,

namely, there is actually no magnetic field outside the solenoid.
For Eq. (
171
), by letting

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}\left(\frac{W_{1}(r,\phi)}{\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-W_{2}(r,\theta)\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)=\dfrac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(178)

we can have

\displaystyle c\,\frac{W_{1}(r,\phi)}{\rho}\hat{e}_{\phi}-\frac{c}{r}W_{2}(r,\theta)\hat{e}_{\theta}=\dfrac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi\rho}\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(179)

In other words, by selecting

\displaystyle W_{1}(r,\phi)=\frac{\Phi_{\rm M}}{2\pi c},\;\;\;\;\;W_{2}(r,\theta)=0,

(180)

one obtains

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{\rm M},\;\;\;\;\;{\rm for}\;\;\;(\rho>r_{0}).

(181)

This fact implies that the magnetic vector potential
\vec{A}_{\rm M}
,
(\rho>r_{0})
, can be derived strictly from the
vector potential extraction approach
.

V.2.2
Example 2

Remark 16
.

Let us consider the case with
G_{r}\neq 0,G_{\theta}\neq 0,G_{\phi}=0
. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\vec{G}=G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}.

(182)

By substituting Eq. (
182
) into Eq. (
161
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{r}\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}+\hat{e}_{\theta}\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\theta}+\hat{e}_{\phi}\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\phi}=\hat{e}_{r}\biggr(\frac{1}{r}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]\biggr),

(183)

which leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}=\frac{1}{r}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr],\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\theta}=0,\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\phi}=0.

(184)

The last two equations of (
16
) implies that
G_{r}
is a function depending only on
r
. We have from the first equation of (
16
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}=\frac{1}{r}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr],

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;G_{r}+r\frac{\partial G_{r}}{\partial r}=2G_{r}+r\frac{\partial G_{r}}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr],

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;G_{r}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]=0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}=-G_{r}\sin\theta,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;\int\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}d\theta=-\int G_{r}\sin\theta d\theta,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;\sin\theta G_{\theta}=-G_{r}\int\sin\theta d\theta,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;\sin\theta G_{\theta}=G_{r}(\cos\theta+C),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;\;G_{\theta}=G_{r}\frac{\cos\theta+C}{\sin\theta},

(185)

where
C
is real constant number.

By substituting Eq. (
16
) into Eq. (
182
), we have

\displaystyle\vec{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}=G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}(-\hat{e}_{r}\times\hat{e}_{\phi})

(186)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}-G_{r}\frac{\cos\theta+C}{\sin\theta}(\hat{e}_{r}\times\hat{e}_{\phi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{r}\frac{\vec{r}}{r}-G_{r}\frac{\cos\theta+C}{r\sin\theta}(\vec{r}\times\hat{e}_{\phi}),

therefore

\displaystyle\vec{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}+q\vec{G}=\vec{r}\times\vec{p}+q\vec{r}\times\left(-G_{r}\frac{\cos\theta+C}{r\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}\right)+qG_{r}\frac{\vec{r}}{r}

(187)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{r}\times\left(\vec{p}-qG_{r}\frac{\cos\theta+C}{r\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}\right)+qG_{r}\frac{\vec{r}}{r}.

\blacksquare

Let us compare Eq. (
187
) with the Wu-Yang angular momentum operator

\vec{L}=\vec{r}\times\left(\vec{p}-Ze\vec{A}\right)-q\frac{\vec{r}}{r},

(188)

we easily find that for

\displaystyle G_{r}=-1,\;\;\;q=Zeg,\;\;\;C=-1,

(189)

one immediately obtains the Wu-Yang monopole vector potential in the region
a
as

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{r}\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\;\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(190)

Similarly, for

\displaystyle G_{r}=-1,\;\;\;q=Zeg,\;\;\;C=1,

(191)

one immediately obtains the Wu-Yang monopole vector potential in the region
b
as

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g}{r}\frac{1+\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\;\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(192)

Thus, we have strictly derived the Wu-Yang U(1) monopole vector potential based on the
vector potential extraction approach
.

Remark 17
.

Let us calculate the corresponding magnetic field. For
\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}

(193)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,A_{\phi})-\frac{\partial A_{\theta}}{\partial\phi}\right]\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{\theta}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\theta})-\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial\theta}\right]\hat{e}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,A_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{\theta}=\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,A_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\sin\theta\,\frac{g}{r}\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\right)\right]\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(1-\cos\theta)\right]\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\sin\theta\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\frac{\vec{r}}{r^{3}}.

For
\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{b}
, we have

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}

(194)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,A_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{r}=\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\sin\theta\,\frac{-g}{r}\frac{1+\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\right)\right]\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-g}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(1+\cos\theta)\right]\hat{e}_{r}=\frac{g}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\sin\theta\hat{e}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\frac{\vec{r}}{r^{3}}.

One finds that the direction of the magnetic field is along the
\hat{e}_{r}
.
\blacksquare

V.2.3
Example 3

Let us look at Eq. (
172
), for the region of
\rho<r_{0}
, in cylindrical coordinate system the vector potential is given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\dfrac{B\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi}=\dfrac{Br\sin\theta}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi}=\dfrac{B\rho}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(195)

or, in Cartesian coordinate system the vector potential is given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{Br\sin\theta}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi}=\dfrac{Br\sin\theta}{2}\left(-\sin\phi\hat{e}_{x}+\cos\phi\hat{e}_{y}\right)

(196)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{B}{2}\left(-r\sin\theta\sin\phi\hat{e}_{x}+r\sin\theta\cos\phi\hat{e}_{y}\right)=\dfrac{B}{2}\left(-y\hat{e}_{x}+x\hat{e}_{y}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{B}{2}\left(y\hat{e}_{x}-x\hat{e}_{y}\right)=-\frac{B}{2}(\vec{r}\times\hat{z}).

In cylindrical coordinate system, one can have the magnetic field as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}

(197)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial A_{z}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial z}\right)\hat{e}_{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial A_{\rho}}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial A_{z}}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{\phi}+\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(\rho A_{\phi})}{\partial\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial A_{\rho}}{\partial\phi}\right)\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial z}\right)\hat{e}_{\rho}+\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(\rho A_{\phi})}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{z}=\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(\rho A_{\phi})}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial(B\rho^{2}/2)}{\partial\rho}\right)\hat{e}_{z}=B\hat{e}_{z}.

Or, in Cartesian coordinate system, one can have the magnetic field as

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}=\dfrac{B}{2}\vec{\nabla}\times\left(-y\hat{e}_{x}+x\hat{e}_{y}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{B}{2}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hat{e}_{x}&\hat{e}_{y}&\hat{e}_{z}\\
\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\\
-y&x&0\end{array}\right|=B\hat{e}_{z}.

namely, the magnetic field is a uniform field along the
z
-direction.

Now, it gives rise to natural question:
Can one derive the vector potential corresponding to the uniform magnetic field along the
z
-direction (without considering the constraint of
\rho<r_{0}
)?
We find that, perhaps due to
some topological reasons
, one is not able to strictly derive the corresponding vector potential, however, one can derive it by taking some reasonable limits from the Wu-Yang U(1) monopole vector potential.

Remark 18
.

The Difficulty of Strict Derivation.
Let us come to show the difficulty of strict derivation.
Based on the following relation

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}\left(G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)=\dfrac{B\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(202)

one can have

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}G_{\theta}=\dfrac{B\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}{2},\;\;\;\;\;\;G_{\phi}=0,

(203)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{c}{r}G_{\theta}=\dfrac{Br\sin\theta}{2},\;\;\;\;\;\;G_{\phi}=0,

(204)

i.e.,

\displaystyle G_{\theta}=\dfrac{Br^{2}\sin\theta}{2c},\;\;\;\;\;\;G_{\phi}=0.

(205)

Let us check the condition (
161
) requiring by the angular momentum, i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})=\vec{r}\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right).

(206)

In spherical coordinate system, we can have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(rG_{r})=\vec{r}\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right),

(207)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(rG_{r})=\vec{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]\right),

(208)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(rG_{r})=\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]\right)r\hat{e}_{r}.

(209)

Due to

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial r}\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(210)

from Eq. (
209
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\theta}=0,\;\;\;\;\;\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial\phi}=0,

(211)

which implies that “
G_{r}
must depend only on
r
”. We then have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}=\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]\right)r,

(212)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}=\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta\frac{Br^{2}\sin\theta}{2c})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]\right)r,

(213)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(rG_{r})}{\partial r}=\frac{1}{r}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{Br^{2}}{2c}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin^{2}\theta)}{\partial\theta}\biggr],

(214)

i.e.,

\displaystyle G_{r}+r\frac{\partial(G_{r})}{\partial r}=2G_{r}+r\frac{\partial(G_{r})}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{Br^{2}}{2c}2\sin\theta\cos\theta,

(215)

i.e.,

\displaystyle G_{r}=-\frac{Br^{2}}{c}\cos\theta.

(216)

However, in Eq. (
216
)
G_{r}
depends on both
r
and
\theta
, which contradicts with Eq. (
209
). Thus, the condition (
206
) is not strictly satisfied.

Let us restrict
r
to the region
\rho<r_{0}
. The condition (
206
) can be satisfied approximately from viewpoint of physics. Because
r_{0}
is the radius of the solenoid, which is very small, i.e.,
r_{0}\approx 0
. When
\theta
tends to
\pi/2
(i.e.,
\cos\theta\rightarrow 0
), one has
r\rightarrow r_{0}
, thus

\displaystyle G_{r}=-\frac{Br^{2}}{c}\cos\theta\approx 0.

(217)

When
\theta\rightarrow 0
(i.e.,
\cos\theta\rightarrow 1
),
r\approx r\cos\theta=z
, by considering the length of the solenoid is finite, i.e.,
z
is finite in practice, then we can approximately have
G_{r}\approx 0
. However, such an approximation may not seem appropriate. Because the orders of
G_{\theta}=\frac{Br^{2}\sin\theta}{2c}
and
G_{r}=-\frac{Br^{2}}{c}\cos\theta
are the same, when
G_{r}\approx 0
, one accordingly have
G_{\theta}\approx 0
. A more suitable approximation is provided in
Example 4
, where the vector potential and the magnetic field of a linear solenoid can be naturally obtained from those of a toroidal solenoid.
\blacksquare

Remark 19
.

Approximation from the Wu-Yang U(1) Monopole Vector Potential.
Our task is derive the vector potential

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{B\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi}=\dfrac{Br\sin\theta}{2}\hat{e}_{\phi}=-\frac{B}{2}(\vec{r}\times\hat{z}),

(218)

and the corresponding uniform magnetic field along the
z
-direction

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}=B\hat{e}_{z}.

(219)

However, the previous remark shows that the strict derivation cannot be achieved. In the following, we would like to derive them approximately from the Wu-Yang U(1) monopole vector potential.

Now, let us study the local approximation of a magnetic monopole near the north pole. Near the north pole of a sphere, the magnetic field and vector potential of a magnetic monopole can be approximated as a uniform magnetic field along the
z
-axis and its corresponding vector potential in symmetric gauge.

Analysis 1.—
Approximation of the Magnetic Field.
The magnetic field of a monopole is given by

\displaystyle\vec{B}=g\,\frac{\vec{r}}{r^{3}},

(220)

where
g
is the magnetic charge. On a sphere of radius
R
, the field is radial.
Near the north pole, i.e., the polar angle
\theta\ll 1
, then one has

\displaystyle\sin\theta\approx\theta,\;\;\;\;\;1-\cos\theta\approx\frac{\theta^{2}}{2},\;\;\;\;\;\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\approx\frac{\theta}{2}.

(221)

We introduce a local Cartesian coordinate system with the
z
-axis pointing radially outward. Then

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{r}\approx\hat{e}_{z},\qquad r\approx R,

(222)

so that

\displaystyle\vec{B}\approx\frac{g}{R^{2}}\,\hat{e}_{z}.

(223)

This is a uniform magnetic field along the
z
-direction, with magnitude
B=g/R^{2}
.

Analysis 2.—
Approximation of the Vector Potential.
The Wu–Yang monopole potential near the north pole is given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}_{a}=\frac{g}{r}\,\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\phi},

(224)

where
\hat{e}_{\phi}=(-\sin\phi,\cos\phi,0)
is the azimuthal unit vector. For small
\theta
,

\displaystyle\frac{1-\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\approx\frac{\theta}{2},\qquad r\approx R.

(225)

On the tangent plane, the cylindrical radius is
\rho=R\sin\theta\approx R\theta
, so
\theta\approx\rho/R
. Substituting,

\displaystyle\vec{A}_{a}\approx\frac{g}{R}\cdot\frac{\rho}{2R}\,\hat{e}_{\phi}=\frac{g\rho}{2R^{2}}\,\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(226)

After expressing
\hat{e}_{\phi}
in Cartesian coordinates:

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{\phi}=\frac{1}{\rho}(-y,x,0),

(227)

we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{A}_{a}\approx\frac{g\rho}{2R^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}(-y,x,0)=\frac{g}{2R^{2}}(-y,x,0).

(228)

Analysis 3.—
Correspondence with the Uniform-Field Vector Potential.

After setting

\displaystyle B=\frac{g}{R^{2}},

(229)

we have

\displaystyle\vec{A}=\frac{B}{2}(-y,x,0),

(230)

which is the symmetric-gauge vector potential for a uniform magnetic field
\vec{B}=B\,\hat{e}_{z}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{A}=B\,\hat{e}_{z}.

(231)

Thus, near the north pole of the sphere, the monopole fields approximate to

\displaystyle\vec{B}\approx\frac{g}{R^{2}}\,\hat{e}_{z},\qquad\vec{A}\approx\frac{g}{2R^{2}}(-y,x,0).

(232)

This ends the derivation.
\blacksquare

Remark 20
.

The other Wu–Yang potential
\vec{A}_{b}
in region b is singular at the north pole and is not used in this region. It can be transformed into
\vec{A}_{a}
by a gauge transformation. This approximation treats the spherical surface locally as a flat plane, neglecting higher-order curvature effects, and is a standard technique in local analysis.
\blacksquare

V.2.4
Example 4

For a toroidal solenoid, the internal magnetic field in spherical coordinates is given by

\displaystyle\vec{B}=\frac{\mu_{0}NI}{2\pi r\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}.

(233)

Here
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum permeability, a fundamental physical constant describing the response of vacuum to a magnetic field, with units H/m (henries per meter);
N
is the total number of turns of the toroidal solenoid, i.e., the total number of windings of the wire;
I
is the current passing through the solenoid wire, in amperes (A);
r
is the radial distance (in spherical coordinates) from the origin (the center of the torus) to a point in space;
\theta
is the polar angle in spherical coordinates, measured from the polar axis (the symmetry axis of the torus). In this formula,
\rho=r\sin\theta
equals the perpendicular distance from the point to the symmetry axis of the toroidal solenoid (i.e., the radial coordinate

\rho
in toroidal coordinates);
\hat{e}_{\phi}
is the unit vector in the azimuthal direction in spherical coordinates, perpendicular to both the radial and polar directions, and pointing along the toroidal direction.

The formula (
233
) describes the magnetic field inside a toroidal solenoid. A toroidal solenoid is a coil wound into a ring shape; its internal magnetic field is completely confined within the toroidal volume, directed tangentially along the torus (i.e., the
\hat{e}_{\phi}

direction). The magnitude is inversely proportional to the perpendicular distance
r\sin\theta
from the symmetry axis and proportional to both the current and the number of turns. The factor
2\pi r\sin\theta
in the denominator is essentially the circumferential length of the magnetic circuit at the given location, reflecting how the magnetic flux density varies with the radial position.

The vector potential of the toroidal solenoid is given by

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+A_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+A_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(234)

with

\displaystyle A_{r}=0,\;\;\;\;\;A_{\theta}=\frac{\mu_{0}NI}{2\pi\sin\theta},\;\;\;\;\;A_{\phi}=0.

(235)

One may check that

\displaystyle\vec{{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{{A}}

(236)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(\sin\theta\,A_{\phi})-\frac{\partial A_{\theta}}{\partial\phi}\right]\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\phi})\right]\hat{e}_{\theta}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\theta})-\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial\theta}\right]\hat{e}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\left[-\frac{\partial A_{\theta}}{\partial\phi}\right]\hat{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\theta})\right]\hat{e}_{\phi}=\frac{1}{r}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(rA_{\theta})\right]\hat{e}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}A_{\theta}\,\hat{e}_{\phi}=\frac{\mu_{0}NI}{2\pi r\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi},

which is just Eq. (
233
).

In the following, let us demonstrate that the vector potential can be determined from the
vector potential extraction approach
. To reach this purpose, we only need to prove such an operator
\vec{G}
exists. Let

\displaystyle\vec{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{r}\hat{e}_{r}+G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta}+G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\phi},

(237)

with

\displaystyle G_{r}=0.

(238)

Then from

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\frac{\vec{r}\times\vec{G}}{r^{2}}=\frac{c}{r}\left(G_{\theta}\hat{e}_{\phi}-G_{\phi}\hat{e}_{\theta}\right)=\frac{\mu_{0}NI}{2\pi\sin\theta}\hat{e}_{\theta},

(239)

one has

\displaystyle G_{\theta}=0,\;\;\;\;\;G_{\phi}=-\frac{\mu_{0}NIr}{2\pi c\sin\theta}.

(240)

Furthermore, we need to check the condition (
161
) requiring by the angular momentum, i.e.,

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(\vec{r}\cdot\vec{G})=\vec{r}\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right).

(241)

In spherical coordinate system, we can have

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}(rG_{r})=\vec{r}\left(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}\right).

(242)

Namely, we need to check

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{G}=0,

(243)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\biggr[\frac{\partial(r^{2}G_{r})}{\partial r}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial(\sin\theta G_{\theta})}{\partial\theta}\biggr]+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]=0,

(244)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\biggr[\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}\biggr]=0,

(245)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}=0,

(246)

which is obviously valid due to Eq. (
240
). This ends the demonstration.

Remark 21
.

Transition from a Toroidal Solenoid to an Infinitely Long Straight Solenoid.

The magnetic field inside a toroidal solenoid is given in spherical coordinates by Eq. (
233
),
where
r\sin\theta=\rho
is the perpendicular distance from the symmetry axis.
When the toroidal radius
R
is very large, the curvature can be neglected locally.
In a small segment near a point on the torus, the toroidal solenoid approximates an infinitely long straight solenoid.
Taking the limit
R\to\infty
while keeping the local geometry, the magnetic field becomes

\displaystyle\vec{B}\approx\frac{\mu_{0}NI}{2\pi R}\,\hat{e}_{\phi}=B\,\hat{e}_{z},

(247)

which is a uniform axial field – the standard field inside an infinite straight solenoid.
Conversely, a topological argument shows that the infinite solenoid (an infinite cylinder) can be compactified by identifying its two ends at infinity; the resulting quotient space is homeomorphic to a torus, i.e., a toroidal solenoid.
Thus, the two systems are physically equivalent in the sense of a local approximation and also topologically related by compactification.
\blacksquare

Remark 22
.

Further Relation between the Infinitely Long Straight Solenoid and the Toroidal Solenoid.

Physically, the magnetic fields of the infinitely long solenoid and the toroidal solenoid are consistent, differing only in their spatial topology. By gluing the points at infinity together, these two models become perfectly unified.

Here, we give a strict proof. For the infinite solenoid (infinite cylinder), it can be written as

\displaystyle C=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta,z)=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)\times(z),\quad\quad(\rho=\text{const},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}).

(248)

Consider the function

\displaystyle f(z)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan z.

(249)

It is a homeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}
to
(-1,1)
. Then we have

\displaystyle C\cong(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)\times(f(z))=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)\times(z_{1}),\quad(\rho=\text{const},\;\theta\in\mathbb{R},\;z_{1}\in(-1,1)).

(250)

After adding the endpoints
\{-1,1\}
, we obtain the compactified cylinder

\displaystyle\bar{C}=(\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta)\times(t),\quad\;\;\;(\rho=\text{const},\;\theta\in\mathbb{R},\;t\in[-1,1]).

(251)

Under this compactification,
z\to-\infty
corresponds to
t=-1
and
z\to+\infty
corresponds to
t=1
.

Now we glue the points at infinity together by defining the equivalence relation

\displaystyle\big((\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta),-1\big)\sim\big((\rho\cos\theta,\rho\sin\theta),1\big),\qquad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}.

(252)

Thus we form the quotient space
